## Tool: custom_tool_call_output

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 14.9 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"a01c8a","wall_time_seconds":7.4493314,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":5614,"output":"\\r\\nIn the LSAT questions, identify the rule or chain that proves each answer. Check that the answer preserves the complete conditions. **Requires approval** does not become **has received approval**. If you cannot prove an answer, try a counterexample: can the given statements be true while the answer is false?\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- END CURRENT STUDENT LESSON V9 -->\\r\\n\\r\\n## 2026-09-30: V10 topic-scope correction\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- BEGIN CURRENT STUDENT LESSON V10 -->\\r\\n\\r\\n# Facts, Rules, and What Follows\\r\\n\\r\\nEvery trained person has a badge. Start with that rule. Here are two different ways to use it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Fact means:** Something established as true about a person or an object. “Mira is trained.”\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule means:** What must be true if a condition is met. “Every trained person has a badge.”\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1 | Apply a rule to a fact\\r\\n\\r\\n**Fact:** Mira is trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** Every trained person has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Conclusion:** Mira has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** Mira meets the rule\'s starting condition: she is trained. The rule tells us she has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Result:** A conclusion about Mira.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2 | Link two rules\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule 1:** Every trained person has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule 2:** Every badge holder has building access.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Conclusion:** Every trained person has building access.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** The first rule gets us from trained to badge. The second gets us from badge to access. The shared condition, has a badge, connects them.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Result:** A new rule. It does not establish that anyone is trained.\\r\\n\\r\\nTreat the statements given in an inference question as true. Use them to prove the conclusion. Do not add an unstated fact or connection.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 01 | Facts state what is true\\r\\n\\r\\nA fact supplied by the question is your starting point. A new fact needs proof from what you were given.\\r\\n\\r\\n“Mira is trained” establishes training for Mira. It does not establish anyone else\'s training, a badge for Mira, or a relationship between training and badges.\\r\\n\\r\\nA fact can also tell us that a condition is absent: **“Niko does not have a badge.”** That is a fact about Niko. Whether it proves anything about his training depends on the rule we are given.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** The east gate is not locked. What is established about the east gate? Does that tell you whether the west gate is locked?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** The east gate is not locked. Nothing here establishes whether the west gate is locked. The fact names one gate; we cannot transfer it to a different gate.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 02 | Rules connect conditions\\r\\n\\r\\nA conditional rule covers **every person or object that meets its starting condition**. It does not establish that anyone or anything meets that condition.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn **“Everyone who is trained has a badge,”** training is the starting condition. Having a badge is the result that must follow for that same person.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- RULE-PARTS -->\\r\\n\\r\\nWrite the conditions as short phrases: **Is trained → Has a badge**. The arrow connects the condition we start with to the result that must follow. If a person is trained, that same person must have a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\nBeing trained is **sufficient** to establish that someone has a badge: it is enough to trigger this rule. Having a badge is **necessary** for being trained under this rule: a trained person cannot lack a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\nThat does not mean training is the only way to get a badge. It also does not establish that anyone is trained. The rule could hold even if no one met its starting condition.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 03 | Apply a rule to the same person or object\\r\\n\\r\\n**Definition:** Applying a rule means finding an established fact that satisfies the rule\'s starting condition, then concluding the required result for **that same person or object**.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Apply the rule forward\\r\\n\\r\\n**Given fact:** Mira is trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** Everyone who is trained has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** “Mira is trained” satisfies the rule\'s condition, “is trained.”\\r\\n\\r\\n**Therefore:** Mira has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** The rule covers every trained person, including Mira. We have proved a new fact about Mira. We have not made a claim about everyone who has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- APPLY-SEQUENCE -->\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every file marked confidential requires restricted storage. This file is marked confidential. What follows? Does the rule alone establish that a confidential file exists?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** This file requires restricted storage. The fact establishes that this particular file is confidential. The rule alone establishes a requirement for any confidential file, without establishing that any such file exists.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 04 | Link rules to produce a new rule\\r\\n\\r\\n**Definition:** Linking rules means matching the result of one rule to the starting condition of another, so that together they establish a **new conditional rule**.\\r\\n\\r\\n### What two rules tell us on their own\\r\\n\\r\\n**First rule:** Every late application requires special approval.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Second rule:** Every application requiring special approval needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- JOIN-RULES -->\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** “Requires special approval” is the result of the first rule and the starting condition of the second. The complete condition matches, not just the word “approval.”\\r\\n\\r\\n**Therefore:** Every late application needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** Consider any application that is late. The first rule requires approval; that satisfies the second rule\'s condition, which requires a signature. This proves the general connection **Submitted late → Needs a supervisor\'s signature**. It does not establish that any application was late.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 05 | Add a fact to the chain\\r\\n\\r\\n**Given fact:** This application was submitted late.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rules:** Submitted late → Requires special approval → Needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Therefore:** This application requires special approval and needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rules-only conclusion was a new **if-then rule**. The fact that this application was late lets us apply the chain to this application. If we have only the rules, we have not established that any application was late.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- TWO-OUTPUTS -->\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every trained person has a badge. Every badge holder has access. No fact identifies a trained person. Can you conclude that Mira has access? What new rule can you conclude?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** You cannot conclude that Mira has access. Nothing establishes that Mira meets the chain\'s starting condition. You can conclude that every trained person has access. That is a new rule, not a fact about Mira.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 06 | Recognize connections the rules do not prove\\r\\n\\r\\n### 1. Do not reverse the rule\\r\\n\\r\\n**Given rule:** Every trained person has a badge. **Is trained → Has a badge**.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Proposed conclusion:** Anyone with a badge is trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Possible example:** An untrained visitor has a badge. The rule still holds: every trained person has a badge. But this badge holder is not trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What follows:** A badge alone does not prove training. Knowing only that someone is not trained also leaves their badge status open. The rule makes no badge claim about untrained people.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 2. A shared starting condition does not connect the results\\r\\n\\r\\nUse only the rules given in this new example. Rules from earlier examples do not carry over.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Given rules:** Every late application requires special approval. Every late application needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- SHARED-TRIGGER -->\\r\\n\\r\\n**Proposed conclusion:** Every application requiring special approval needs a supervisor\'s signature.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Possible example:** An on-time application requires approval for another reason, without needing a signature. Neither rule is broken. They only tell us what late applications require.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What follows:** If an application is late, both rules apply to it. It requires approval and needs a signature. Approval alone does not prove that it needs a signature.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- POP-QUIZ-2 -->\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3. A shared result does not connect the starting conditions\\r\\n\\r\\n**Given rules:** Confidential files require restricted storage. Medical files require restricted storage.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- SHARED-END -->\\r\\n\\r\\n**Proposed conclusion:** Every confidential file is a medical file.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Possible example:** A confidential financial report requires restricted storage but is not a medical file. Restricted storage can cover both kinds of files separately.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What follows:** Neither type of file has been shown to belong to the other. Ending at the same condition does not create a link between their starting conditions.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Check the whole condition before linking\\r\\n\\r\\nFind the result of the first rule and the starting condition of the second. They must match, including any negation or qualifier. If they do not match, these rules do not form a chain as written. Keep them separate.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every member of a review panel has training. Every member of that panel has a security badge. Must everyone with training have a security badge? What follows if Dana is a panel member?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** Training alone does not guarantee a badge. A trained person could be outside the panel. Because Dana is a panel member, both rules apply to Dana: Dana has training and a security badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 07 | Use the contrapositive when the result is absent\\r\\n\\r\\nA different fact calls for a different move. Suppose **Niko does not have a badge**, and the same rule says **everyone who is trained has a badge**.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf Niko were trained, the rule would require Niko to have a badge. That would contradict the given fact. So **Niko is not trained**.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Given fact:** Niko does not have a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** Is trained → Has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** The fact denies the required result. Restate the rule as **No badge → Not trained**.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Therefore:** Niko is not trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** Training cannot be present while the rule\'s required result is absent. This is the **contrapositive**: reverse the arrow and negate both complete conditions.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe contrapositive states the same rule. It does not deny the original rule, and it is not the unsupported reversal **Has a badge → Is trained**.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- TWO-MOVES -->\\r\\n\\r\\n### Four facts, one unchanged rule\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule is **Is trained → Has a badge**. The rule stays the same. Change only what we know about the person.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Fact 1: The person is trained\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** The original starting condition is met.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What follows:** The person has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** Every trained person has a badge under this rule. Apply it to this person.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Fact 2: The person has no badge\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** The required result is absent. This meets the contrapositive\'s starting condition.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What follows:** The person is not trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** A trained person without a badge would contradict the rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Fact 3: The person has a badge\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** We have only the original result. The rule does not say that everyone with a badge is trained.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What follows:** Whether this person is trained remains unknown.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** An untrained visitor with a badge fits the rule. A trained person with a badge also fits it.\\r\\n\\r\\n#### Fact 4: The person is not trained\\r\\n\\r\\n**Match:** The original starting condition is absent. The rule only guarantees badges for trained people.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What follows:** Whether this person has a badge remains unknown.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why:** An untrained person could have a badge or lack one. Both situations fit the rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Trained, but no badge** contradicts the rule. The other three combinations fit.\\r\\n\\r\\nNow explore those same moves with a different rule: **Every late application requires special approval.** First identify the supplied fact. Then see whether it triggers the original rule, triggers its contrapositive, or leaves the other condition open.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- FACT-LAB -->\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every confidential file requires restricted storage. This file does not require restricted storage. What follows? What if all you know is that it does require restricted storage?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** If it does not require restricted storage, it is not confidential. That is the contrapositive. If it does require restricted storage, its confidentiality remains unknown. A nonconfidential file could require restricted storage for another reason.\\r\\n\\r\\nYou do not have to write every contrapositive automatically. If the fact clearly meets the original starting condition, apply that rule. If the fact denies its result, or a connection is difficult to see, a contrapositive can make the supported move clear.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Carry a negative fact through a chain\\r\\n\\r\\nReturn to the two application rules: **Submitted late → Requires special approval → Needs a supervisor\'s signature**.\\r\\n\\r\\nNow add a different fact: **This application does not need a supervisor\'s signature.** If this application required special approval, the second rule would require a signature. So it does not require approval. If it were late, the first rule would require approval. So it was not late.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Contrapositive chain:** Does not need signature → Does not require approval → Was not late.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Therefore:** This application does not require special approval and was not late.\\r\\n\\r\\n### A contrapositive can uncover the match\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose these two rules are given:\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule 1:** Is trained → Has a badge.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule 2:** Not trained → Requires supervision.\\r\\n\\r\\n“Trained” and “not trained” are opposite ideas in the same position: both are starting conditions. They do not look like a chain yet. Take the contrapositive of the first rule:\\r\\n\\r\\n**Restated rule 1:** No badge → Not trained.\\r\\n\\r\\nNow “not trained” is the result of that restatement and the starting condition of rule 2. We can link them:\\r\\n\\r\\n**New chain:** No badge → Not trained → Requires supervision.\\r\\n\\r\\n**New conditional:** Anyone without a badge requires supervision.\\r\\n\\r\\nAgain, that is a general rule. It does not prove that someone lacks a badge. If a separate fact says Niko lacks a badge, then we can apply the chain and conclude that Niko requires supervision.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- HIDDEN-CHAIN -->\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the middle condition already matches, link the rules directly. You only need to restate a rule when doing so reveals a connection you could not use as written.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 08 | Translate the complete claim\\r\\n\\r\\n### “Only if” introduces the required condition\\r\\n\\r\\n“A visitor may enter only if the visitor has a badge” means permission to enter requires a badge. Write **May enter → Has a badge**. Put the requirement after the arrow. A badge alone does not prove permission to enter.\\r\\n\\r\\n“Only badge holders may enter” states that same requirement. Translate the complete sentence before shortening its conditions.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Keep requirements separate from completed actions\\r\\n\\r\\n“Requires special approval” and “has received special approval” are not the same condition. A requirement can remain unfulfilled. A rule ending in the first phrase cannot be linked to one starting in the second unless another statement connects them. Shared words are not enough; match the complete claim, including qualifiers and negation.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** Every late application requires special approval. Every application that has received special approval has a supervisor\'s signature. Does a late application necessarily have a supervisor\'s signature? Explain the missing connection.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** No. A late application requires approval, but it may not have received it. The second rule applies to applications that have received approval. Nothing says the late application\'s requirement has been fulfilled.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- CHAIN-LAB -->\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Rules can connect different kinds of claims\\r\\n\\r\\nA rule can tell us what someone **should** do or what they **may** do. That is different from telling us what they actually did.\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose **every damaged item should be inspected**. If this item is damaged, it should be inspected. We cannot conclude it has been inspected. But “has not been inspected” is not the same as “it is not true that it should be inspected.” An inspection could be overdue.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- MODALITY -->\\r\\n\\r\\nFor **Damaged → Should be inspected**, the contrapositive starts with “it is not true that it should be inspected” and concludes “not damaged.” Negate the complete claim about what should happen. Whether the item has actually been inspected is a different claim.\\r\\n\\r\\nSuppose **a visitor may enter the archive only if the visitor has a badge**. A badge is necessary for permission to enter. The rule is **May enter → Has a badge**. Without a badge, the visitor does not have permission to enter under this rule. Having a badge alone does not establish permission; another requirement might still apply.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule concerns permission. It does not prove that someone physically cannot enter without permission. Keep the same kind of claim throughout your reasoning: a requirement stays a requirement, advice stays advice, and permission stays permission.\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n### Negate the whole requirement\\r\\n\\r\\nA condition can contain more than one part. Suppose **a document may be released only if it has been reviewed AND authorized**. Permission requires both parts. If either part is missing, the combined requirement is not satisfied.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** May be released → Reviewed AND authorized.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Contrapositive:** Not reviewed OR not authorized → May not be released.\\r\\n\\r\\nTo negate “reviewed AND authorized,” you only need at least one failure. You do not need both parts to fail. “Not both” does not mean “neither.”\\r\\n\\r\\nNow suppose **a visitor may enter only if the visitor has a badge OR an invitation**. This requirement allows either route, including both. To fail the entire requirement, the visitor must have neither.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Rule:** May enter → Has badge OR has invitation.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Contrapositive:** No badge AND no invitation → May not enter.\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- NEGATION -->\\r\\n\\r\\nNegating a compound condition changes **AND to OR**, or **OR to AND**, while negating each part. For the original AND requirement, one missing part is enough. For the original OR requirement, both routes must be missing.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Try it:** A document may be released only if it has been reviewed and authorized. It has been reviewed but not authorized. What follows? Does the contrapositive require us to conclude it was not reviewed?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** The document may not be released. Missing authorization is enough to fail the combined requirement. We do not conclude that review is missing too; the fact says review occurred.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 09 | Choose the move and test the claim\\r\\n\\r\\nAsk what you have: a fact that can activate a rule, or two rules that can connect? The matching condition determines the move.\\r\\n\\r\\n| What you have | The move | What to check |\\r\\n| --- | --- | --- |\\r\\n| A fact meeting a rule\'s starting condition | Apply the rule forward | Does the whole fact match the whole condition? |\\r\\n| A fact denying a rule\'s required result | Use the contrapositive | Did you reverse and negate both complete ideas? |\\r\\n| Two rules with a matching middle condition | Link the rules | Does the first rule end with the exact condition the second starts with? |\\r\\n| Only the required result, or only the absence of the starting condition | Leave the other side open | Could another explanation fit the rule? |\\r\\n\\r\\nTo disprove a proposed inference, keep every given statement true and make the proposed conclusion false. For example, an on-time application that requires approval fits **Submitted late → Requires approval**, but disproves **Requires approval → Submitted late**.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 10 | Mix the moves\\r\\n\\r\\nUse only the information given in each problem. For each problem, write what follows and name the move. If a proposed conclusion is not guaranteed, explain a situation that would make it false while keeping the given statements true.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Practice 1:** Every certified instructor has a license. Every person who is not a certified instructor requires supervision. Quinn has no license. What follows? Show the connection between the rules.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** Quinn is not a certified instructor and requires supervision. Contrapose the first rule: no license → not a certified instructor. That result meets the second rule\'s starting condition. The chain is no license → not certified → requires supervision. Quinn\'s fact activates it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Practice 2:** A visitor may enter a lab only if the visitor has a pass or an invitation. Sasha has no pass and no invitation. What follows? If Sasha had an invitation, would that alone establish permission to enter?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** Sasha may not enter. Permission requires at least one of the two alternatives, and Sasha has neither. An invitation alone would not prove permission: the rule says what permission requires, not that meeting this requirement is sufficient.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Practice 3:** Every flagged expense requires manager approval. Every expense that has received manager approval may be reimbursed. This expense is flagged. Must this expense be eligible for reimbursement?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** No. This expense requires approval, but nothing establishes that it has received approval. It could still be waiting for the manager. The second rule\'s starting condition is not established, so the two phrases cannot serve as a matching middle condition.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Practice 4:** Every archive worker knows the catalog. Every archive worker has a key. Sam knows the catalog. Must Sam have a key? What changes if you also learn that Sam is an archive worker?\\r\\n\\r\\n**Worked answer:** Catalog knowledge alone does not prove Sam has a key. Sam could know the catalog without working in the archive and have no key. If Sam is an archive worker, both rules apply to Sam: Sam knows the catalog and has a key. Shared starting conditions support both results for a person who meets the starting condition, without connecting the results to each other.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 11 | Take this into your assigned work\\r\\n\\r\\nDo the **conditional-diagramming drills first**, then move to the **LSAT questions**. In the drills, write the complete conditions before shortening them. Check your arrow and its contrapositive. When two rules appear, look for an exact match before combining them.\\r\\n\\r\\nIn the LSAT questions, identify the rule or chain that proves each answer. Check that the answer preserves the complete conditions. **Requires approval** does not become **has received approval**. If you cannot prove an answer, try a counterexample: can the given statements be true while the answer is false?\\r\\n\\r\\n<!-- END CURRENT STUDENT LESSON V10 -->\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"989d0e","wall_time_seconds":7.0313404,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2035,"output":"---\\r\\ntitle: Conditional Must Be True · Facts, Rules, and Bridges\\r\\ntype: recap\\r\\nrecord_kind: primary\\r\\nstudent: sofia\\r\\nsession_date: 2026-09-11\\r\\nsource: transcript-downloads/2026-09-11-sofia-notes-by-gemini.md\\r\\nstatus: draft\\r\\nposted: false\\r\\nposted_at:\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Conditional Must Be True: Facts, Rules, and Bridges\\r\\n\\r\\nA conditional Must Be True question has two basic moves. You either connect one rule to another, or use a fact to activate a rule. Before you combine anything, identify which kind of move the stimulus gives you.\\r\\n\\r\\nUnlike an argument question, you are not looking for a conclusion to challenge. Accept every statement as true. Your only question is: **what new information do these statements guarantee?**\\r\\n\\r\\n## The method at a glance\\r\\n\\r\\n| Pieces you have | Move | What you produce |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Rule + rule | **Chaining** | A new rule |\\r\\n| Fact + rule | **Triggering** | A new fact |\\r\\n\\r\\nDo not start diagramming everything together. First separate the rules from the facts. Then ask whether you should be chaining or triggering.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 1. Know what each statement does\\r\\n\\r\\n| Type | What it does | Example |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| **Fact** | Describes the situation you actually have | Lena is a pilot. |\\r\\n| **Rule** | States what must follow if a condition is met | `pilot → trained` |\\r\\n\\r\\nA rule describes a relationship. It tells you what follows **if** its trigger is met. A fact tells you what is true in the situation. The rule does not prove its own trigger. You need a fact to do that.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Look for certainty, not just a keyword\\r\\n\\r\\nWords such as **if**, **only**, **all**, **every**, and **none** often signal a rule because they create a guarantee. Words such as **often**, **can**, **some**, and **most** do not create that same guarantee by themselves.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe real test is simple: if the first part is true, **must** the second part follow? If not, you do not have a conditional rule.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 2. Chaining: rule + rule produces a new rule\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen the stimulus gives you several rules and no fact that activates them, look for a **bridge**.\\r\\n\\r\\nA bridge is the overlapping idea that sits at the end of one rule and the beginning of another. It is the middle step that lets you move from the first idea to the last.\\r\\n\\r\\nA usable bridge appears in one of two ways:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. The **same idea appears in opposite positions**: the result of one rule and the trigger of another.\\r\\n2. **Opposite versions of an idea appear in the same position**: taking a contrapositive will place the shared idea in opposite positions.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 1: Diagram each rule\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\npilot → trained\\r\\ntrained → licensed\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 2: Find the overlapping idea\\r\\n\\r\\n`trained` is the bridge because it is:\\r\\n\\r\\n- the result of the first rule; and\\r\\n- the trigger of the second rule.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe first rule takes you **to** trained. The second rule takes you **from** trained. The two rules interlock there.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Step 3: Connect the loose ends\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\npilot → licensed\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThe bridge is used to build the inference, but it usually disappears from the new rule. The conclusion connects the two ideas left on the outside: `pilot` and `licensed`.\\r\\n\\r\\n### A repeated word is not always a bridge\\r\\n\\r\\nThe position has to work:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nA → B\\r\\nB → C\\r\\n\\r\\nValid chain: A → C\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nBut this does not chain:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nA → B\\r\\nC → B\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nBoth rules end at `B`. Neither one continues from it. The same idea appears twice, but the rules do not interlock.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Sometimes the bridge is hidden in a contrapositive\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the ideas almost line up, write the valid contrapositive and check again:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\npilot → trained\\r\\nnot licensed → not trained\\r\\n\\r\\nContrapositive of the second rule:\\r\\ntrained → licensed\\r\\n\\r\\nNow they chain:\\r\\npilot → trained → licensed\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nContraposing can reveal a bridge. Reversing a rule cannot.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The answer may test any link you had to build\\r\\n\\r\\nWith a longer chain, the answer does not have to connect the very first and very last ideas. If the chain is:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nA → B → C → D\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nthen `A → C`, `B → D`, and `A → D` are all valid inferences. The test can reward any connection that required you to move through at least one bridge.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 3. Triggering: fact + rule produces a new fact\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen the stimulus gives you a rule and a fact, compare the fact with both sides of the rule. The fact can activate the rule in one of two valid ways.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The fact confirms the trigger\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nRule: pilot → trained\\r\\nFact: Lena is a pilot.\\r\\n\\r\\nTherefore: Lena is trained.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThe fact places Lena on the left side of the rule. Once the trigger is established, the result must follow.\\r\\n\\r\\n### The fact denies the required result\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nRule: pilot → trained\\r\\nFact: Lena is not trained.\\r\\n\\r\\nTherefore: Lena is not a pilot.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThis uses the contrapositive. Reverse the rule and negate both sides:\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\npilot → trained\\r\\nnot trained → not pilot\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n### The two facts that do not activate the rule\\r\\n\\r\\nStarting rule: `pilot → trained`\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nFact: Lena is trained.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThat does not prove she is a pilot. She could have received training for another reason.\\r\\n\\r\\n```text\\r\\nFact: Lena is not a pilot.\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\nThat does not prove she is untrained. She could still be trained for something else.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe rule has no power in either situation. Do not force an inference.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 4. Know the four possible moves\\r\\n\\r\\nStarting rule: `A → B`\\r\\n\\r\\n| What the fact establishes | What you may conclude | Result |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| `A` | `B` | Valid: apply the rule forward |\\r\\n| `not B` | `not A` | Valid: use the contrapositive |\\r\\n| `B` | Nothing about `A` | Invalid reversal |\\r\\n| `not A` | Nothing about `B` | Invalid negation |\\r\\n\\r\\n### Use the contrapositive when the move is hidden\\r\\n\\r\\nYou do not need to write the contrapositive of every rule forever. If the chain or trigger is already clear, use it. If you are staring at the rules and cannot see what to do, contrapose one of them and check again. The missing connection will often become visible.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Diagram when the notation will do work\\r\\n\\r\\nDiagram when you have several rules, a longer chain, or answer choices that are difficult to track in words. The notation should help you:\\r\\n\\r\\n- see which ideas can actually connect;\\r\\n- test an intermediate link in a longer chain;\\r\\n- catch an answer that reverses or negates a rule; or\\r\\n- take the contrapositive of the whole chain.\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the valid move is already clear from the sentence, make it and keep going. The diagram is a tool for making the relationship visible, not an extra task you must perform on every question.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Test a conditional answer by its trigger\\r\\n\\r\\nWhen an answer choice gives you a new conditional rule, start with its trigger. Ask:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Does this idea appear as a trigger anywhere in the stimulus or in a valid inference?\\r\\n2. If not, could a contrapositive place it there?\\r\\n3. Can the proposed result actually be reached from that trigger?\\r\\n\\r\\nIf the answer begins from a condition the stimulus never establishes in that position, the answer cannot be derived. Eliminate it. This is faster and more reliable than trying to make the whole answer sound reasonable.\\r\\n\\r\\n## 5. Use the same process every time\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Label each statement as a **fact** or a **rule**.\\r\\n2. If you have **rule + rule**, look for an overlapping idea that lets the rules interlock.\\r\\n3. If you have **fact + rule**, check whether the fact confirms the trigger or denies the required result.\\r\\n4. Write only the inference the rule guarantees.\\r\\n5. Check the answer choices for that inference. For a conditional answer, test its trigger first. If an answer reverses or negates the rule, eliminate it.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Practice\\r\\n\\r\\n- [ ] Complete the assigned conditional-diagramming practice. Label the trigger and result, then write the contrapositive until that move becomes automatic.\\r\\n- [ ] For each stimulus, write **chaining** or **triggering** before solving it.\\r\\n- [ ] Repeat the Read, Recall, Colour exercise after reviewing the instructions and checking the microphone.\\r\\n- [ ] Complete one Reading Comprehension passage and one Logical Reasoning section.\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

text(await tools.apply_patch("*** Begin Patch\n*** Update File: C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os-projects/lsatu-conditional-bootcamp/src/bootcamps/conditionals/content/foundations.source.ts\n@@\n  * Understanding, intuition technique when stuck, and 6 step process notes.\n+ * Fact/rule contrasts follow Yahunatan's current V10 recap and Sofia's\n+ * 2026-09-11 Conditional MBT draft. Their local draft status is not proof of posting.\n+ * Recall and Rephrase instructions are an authored adaptation of archived homework\n+ * naming that exercise; the archive does not supply its complete original procedure.\n@@\n       'Two rules can create a new rule. Digitized → searchable and searchable → indexed give digitized → indexed. They do not establish that an indexed map exists. Add a digitized case as a separate fact to establish an indexed case.',\n+      'Make the reasoning visible: Fact: Map R is digitized. Rule: every digitized map is searchable. Match: the fact meets the complete digitized-map condition for R. Conclusion: R is searchable. Why: the rule covers that same map. Contrast this with two rules alone: digitized → searchable → indexed proves a new rule, without proving a fact about R.',\n+      'Keep one rule unchanged and vary only the fact: translated notice → archived notice. Notice H is translated: H is archived. H is not archived: H is not translated. H is archived: translation remains unknown. H is not translated: archiving remains unknown. In the last two cases, give an allowed example rather than forcing a conclusion.',\n+      'Requires approval does not mean has received approval. A late submission may require approval while it is still waiting for review. If the next rule begins with received approval, the requirement does not activate it. Preserve the whole condition before comparing the fact and rule.',\n@@\n-    guidedExample: question('rules-facts-guide', 'Every translated notice is archived. Notice H is archived. What follows about translation?', ['H is translated.', 'H is not translated.', 'Translation is unknown.'], 2, 'Archiving is the required result, not the trigger.', ['Translated notices must be archived.', 'Other notices may also be archived, so the fact settles neither translation nor its absence.']),\n+    guidedExample: question('rules-facts-guide', 'Every translated notice is archived. Notice H is archived. Before choosing, identify the fact, rule, and match. State your conclusion and explain why it follows or remains open. What follows about translation?', ['H is translated.', 'H is not translated.', 'Translation is unknown.'], 2, 'Archiving is the required result, not the trigger. An untranslated archived notice would fit the rule.', ['Translated notices must be archived.', 'Other notices may also be archived, so the fact settles neither translation nor its absence.']),\n@@\n       'The six-step process finishes by translating back to English. Do not stop at symbols. For listed → not discontinued, its contrapositive is discontinued → not listed. Say it plainly: if this product is discontinued, it is not listed. That sentence checks both the negative and the direction.',\n+      'Use the six steps to learn and check translation. They are a training scaffold, not six extra tasks you must write on every LSAT question forever. Apply an obvious forward match directly. Write the contrapositive when the fact denies a result or when it exposes a hidden connection. Use notation when it makes the relationship easier to track.',\n@@\n       'Build the chain visibly before compressing it. Write calibrated → registered and registered → listed on separate lines. Align the repeated registered condition so that the first arrow arrives where the next arrow begins. Follow the path to calibrated → listed. Keep branches in the available space; a shared result with two incoming arrows is not a forward bridge.',\n+      'A longer chain supports intermediate connections too. For calibrated → registered → listed → indexed, calibrated → listed, registered → indexed, and calibrated → indexed all follow. An answer need not connect the first and last ideas. Start with the proposed answer\\'s trigger and check whether a forward path reaches its exact result.',\n+      'Compare these complete middle conditions: late submission → requires approval; received approval → issued a reference number. They do not link. Requires approval and received approval differ, even though approval appears in both. A late submission still awaiting approval fits both rules without a reference number, so that proposed conclusion is not guaranteed.',\n@@\n       'An apparent mismatch may be real or may be resolved by a valid contrapositive. Check exact scope and subjects before declaring no link. The preceding linking lesson showed how contraposition exposes hidden connections. Use that procedure before deciding that a shared word supplies no valid path.',\n+      'Check the requirement-versus-completion distinction explicitly. Late application → requires approval does not meet received approval → has a signature. A late application awaiting approval is a counterexample to the claim that it must have a signature. Nothing in the two rules says its requirement has been fulfilled.',\n*** End Patch"));

It includes unless, until and without in their conditional uses. The audit proceeds unless its authorization is withdrawn means not withdrawn → proceeds. Its contrapositive is not proceeds → withdrawn.\',\\r\\n      \'You can choose either complete idea, negate it and make it sufficient, while keeping the other unchanged. For proceeds unless authorization withdrawn, negating proceeds gives not proceeds → authorization withdrawn. The two forms are contrapositives, not competing interpretations.\',\\r\\n      \'Preserve negatives already in the sentence. The gate does not open without a clearance means no clearance → no opening, equivalent to opening → clearance. Until also sets a time boundary: it does not promise that the withheld action happens immediately after the boundary. Without can merely describe circumstances, so first verify what the sentence actually guarantees.\',\\r\\n    ],\\r\\n    method: [\'Identify the conditional indicator and Group 3, checking its meaning and target.\', \'Identify the two complete ideas, including existing negatives, subjects and any time boundary.\', \'Assign symbols to the complete ideas without dropping their qualifiers.\', \'Apply the Group 3 rule: negate one idea on the left and keep the other unchanged on the right; cancel any resulting double negative.\', \'Find the equivalent contrapositive by reversing and negating both complete conditions.\', \'Translate both forms back to English and check that neither invents a guarantee from the exception.\'],\\r\\n    workedExample: { statements: [\'The meeting proceeds unless its venue becomes unavailable.\'], reasoning: [\'Choose venue becomes unavailable and negate it.\', \'No venue unavailability → meeting proceeds.\', \'The equivalent direction is meeting does not proceed → venue becomes unavailable.\'], conclusion: \'A venue becoming unavailable does not by itself prove cancellation.\' },\\r\\n    guidedExample: question(\'group-3-guide\', \'The pump fails without coolant. Which is guaranteed?\', [\'Coolant present → pump does not fail.\', \'No coolant → pump fails.\', \'Pump fails → no coolant.\'], 1, \'Without supplies the absent condition that triggers failure.\', [\'Coolant absence is sufficient for failure.\', \'Presence may still coexist with failure from another cause.\']),\\r\\n    commonMistake: { mistake: \'Making unless exclusive or treating the exception as guaranteed success.\', repair: \'Translate the stated guarantee and its contrapositive. Do not add a reverse or a promise about what happens once the exception occurs.\' },\\r\\n    drills: {\\r\\n      beginner: [\\r\\n        question(\'group-3-b1\', \'The delivery proceeds unless the road closes. Translate.\', [\'Road not closed → delivery proceeds.\', \'Road closed → delivery does not proceed.\', \'Delivery proceeds → road not closed.\'], 0, \'Negate road closes and keep proceeds unchanged.\', [\'The rule promises delivery when closure is absent.\', \'It does not establish the outcome when closure occurs.\']),\\r\\n        question(\'group-3-b2\', \'The display dims without external power. Which follows?\', [\'External power → no dimming.\', \'No external power → display dims.\', \'Display dims → no external power.\'], 1, \'Start with absence of external power.\', [\'Without external power supplies the sufficient condition.\', \'The result is dimming, without excluding other causes.\']),\\r\\n        question(\'group-3-b3\', \'The briefing ends unless another speaker arrives. No other speaker arrives. What follows?\', [\'The briefing ends.\', \'The briefing continues.\', \'A speaker must arrive.\'], 0, \'Use the nonexception case.\', [\'No arrival meets the negated exception.\', \'The promised ending follows.\']),\\r\\n      ],\\r\\n      medium: [\\r\\n        question(\'group-3-m1\', \'The ticket is not issued without identification. Which is equivalent?\', [\'Ticket issued → identification supplied.\', \'Identification supplied → ticket issued.\', \'Ticket not issued → no identification.\'], 0, \'First write no identification → not issued.\', [\'Negating the not-issued result gives issued.\', \'Its contrapositive requires identification.\']),\\r\\n        question(\'group-3-m2\', \'Unless the upload fails, the receipt appears. No receipt appears. What follows?\', [\'The upload fails.\', \'The upload succeeds.\', \'The upload was never attempted.\'], 0, \'Use the contrapositive of no failure → receipt appears.\', [\'Receipt absence excludes no upload failure.\', \'It therefore establishes failure under the stated rule, not a different explanation.\']),\\r\\n        question(\'group-3-m3\', \'Lena does not sign until the price is fixed. The price has been fixed. What follows?\', [\'Lena has signed.\', \'Lena cannot sign.\', \'Whether Lena signs is still unknown.\'], 2, \'Removing…335 tokens truncated…general guarantee here?\', [\'The sentence describes the circumstances of a particular visit.\', \'It supplies no exceptionless rule about other visits.\']),\\r\\n      ],\\r\\n    },\\r\\n  },\\r\\n  {\\r\\n    id: \'group-4\', title: \'Group 4: Keep Two Conditions Apart\', area: \'translation\', phase: \'conditional\', prerequisites: [\'group-3\'],\\r\\n    goal: \'Translate exclusions as a sufficient condition requiring the other condition\\\\\'s absence.\',\\r\\n    teaching: [\\r\\n      \'Group 4 in the Refresher is negate necessary: no, none, not both, never and cannot in their relevant uses. No archived sample is disposable gives archived sample → not disposable. Choosing disposable as the trigger gives disposable → not archived sample.\',\\r\\n      \'Not both says two conditions cannot occur together. The west hatch and the east hatch are not both open gives west open → east not open and east open → west not open. It permits both to be closed. It does not require exactly one to be open.\',\\r\\n      \'Never and cannot need context. A descriptive rule that the device never runs uncooled excludes that combination. An instruction that a visitor cannot enter means entry is forbidden; it does not physically prevent an unauthorized entry. Keep the claim\\\\\'s modal meaning rather than changing permission into behavior.\',\\r\\n    ],\\r\\n    method: [\'Identify the exclusion indicator and Group 4, checking exactly what coexistence is forbidden.\', \'Identify the two complete conditions that cannot coexist, preserving subjects, qualifiers and existing negatives.\', \'Assign symbols to those complete conditions.\', \'Apply the Group 4 rule: keep one unchanged on the left and negate the other on the right; cancel any resulting double negative.\', \'Find the equivalent contrapositive by reversing and negating both complete conditions.\', \'Translate both forms back to English and check that the rule excludes coexistence without inventing existence or an exactly-one requirement.\'],\\r\\n    workedExample: { statements: [\'No reusable vial is marked for disposal.\'], reasoning: [\'Reusable vial → not marked for disposal.\', \'Marked for disposal → not reusable vial is equivalent.\'], conclusion: \'The two categories do not overlap; neither category is asserted to have members.\' },\\r\\n    guidedExample: question(\'group-4-guide\', \'The front pump and rear pump are not both active. Ask whether the rule also supplied an at-least-one claim; if it did not, allow neither.\' },\\r\\n    drills: {\\r\\n      beginner: [\\r\\n        question(\'group-4-b1\', \'No rented instrument is for sale. What follows for an overnight shipment?\', [\'It is uninsured.\', \'It is insured.\', \'It is delayed.\'], 1, \'Negate uninsured as not insured.\', [\'Overnight shipment → not uninsured.\', \'Using uninsured to mean not insured, the double negative yields insured.\']),\\r\\n        question(\'group-4-b3\', \'A recorder never stores an unverified clip. Which follows?\', [\'Stored clip → verified clip.\', \'Verified clip → stored clip.\', \'Unstored clip → unverified clip.\'], 0, \'Storage excludes being unverified.\', [\'The forbidden pair is stored and unverified.\', \'A stored clip therefore must be verified under this descriptive rule.\']),\\r\\n      ],\\r\\n      medium: [\\r\\n        question(\'group-4-m1\', \'The gallery and workshop are not both open. The gallery is closed. What follows about the workshop?\', [\'It is open.\', \'It is closed.\', \'Its status is unknown.\'], 2, \'Only joint opening is excluded.\', [\'The gallery\\\\\'s closure removes the prohibited combination.\', \'The workshop may be open or closed.\']),\\r\\n        question(\'group-4-m2\', \'No locked folder is publicly readable. Folder C is publicly readable. What follows?\', [\'C is locked.\', \'C is not locked.\', \'Every unlocked folder is public.\'], 1, \'Use the equivalent exclusion from the other direction.\', [\'Public readability cannot coexist with locking.\', \'C\\\\\'s readability therefore excludes locking.\']),\\r\\n        question(\'group-4-m3\', \'No metal tool approved for this scan is magnetic. One word tells you which is which.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <div class=\\"cs-specimen\\">\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-specimen-row\\">\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-half\\">\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-mono cs-half-word\\">trigger</div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-half-gloss\\">what sets the rule off</div>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n      <svg width=\\"86\\" height=\\"26\\" viewBox=\\"0 0 86 26\\" fill=\\"none\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\" style=\\"flex:none;\\">\\r\\n        <path d=\\"M2 13h74\\" stroke=\\"var(--classroom-link)\\" stroke-width=\\"2.4\\" stroke-linecap=\\"round\\"/>\\r\\n        <path d=\\"M68 5l9 8-9 8\\" stroke=\\"var(--classroom-link)\\" stroke-width=\\"2.4\\" stroke-linecap=\\"round\\" stroke-linejoin=\\"round\\"/>\\r\\n      </svg>\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-half\\">\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-mono cs-half-word\\">requirement</div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-half-gloss\\">what has to be there</div>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-rule\\" style=\\"margin-top:26px;\\"></div>\\r\\n    <p class=\\"cs-specimen-note\\">Read every rule as the left requires the right, and the ones that link get easy to spot.</p>\\r\\n  </div>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <h2 class=\\"cs-eyebrowless\\">There are only four moves</h2>\\r\\n  <p class=\\"cs-lede\\">Every word on this page does one of them. Find the word, do what it says.</p>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <div class=\\"cs-grid-2\\" style=\\"margin-bottom:44px;\\">\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-card\\">\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-card-head\\"><span class=\\"cs-num\\">01</span><span class=\\"cs-card-title\\">Left</span></div>\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-notation\\">A &rarr; B</div>\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-card-gloss\\">The word\'s idea is the trigger. It goes on the left, exactly as written.</div>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-card\\">\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-card-head\\"><span class=\\"cs-num\\">02</span><span class=\\"cs-card-title\\">Right</span></div>\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-notation\\">B &rarr; A</div>\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-card-gloss\\">The word\'s idea is what is required. It goes on the right, exactly as written.</div>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-card\\">\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-card-head\\"><span class=\\"cs-num\\">03</span><span class=\\"cs-card-title\\">Negate left</span></div>\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-notation\\">B &rarr; A, and a NOT</div>\\r\\n      <di…2958 tokens truncated…</div></div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-word\\">will</div><div class=\\"cs-example\\"><div class=\\"cs-example-sentence\\">If the deadline passes, the form will lock.</div><div class=\\"cs-example-arrow\\">deadline passes &rarr; form locks</div></div></div>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n    </details>\\r\\n  </div>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <!-- NEGATE LEFT -->\\r\\n  <div class=\\"cs-family\\" id=\\"cs-negleft\\">\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-tagline\\"><span class=\\"cs-tag\\">NEGATE LEFT</span><span class=\\"cs-count\\">4 words</span></div>\\r\\n    <h2 class=\\"cs-h2\\">Words that negate the left</h2>\\r\\n    <p class=\\"cs-why\\">All four are \\"if not\\" in disguise. The attached idea is what is required, so it goes right, and the other side picks up a NOT.</p>\\r\\n    <p class=\\"cs-ask\\">If you would rather not negate anything, swap the word for \\"if not\\" and put that on the left instead. Both routes give the same rule, one as the flip of the other.</p>\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-worked\\">\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-worked-sentence\\">You cannot enter unless you have a pass.</div>\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-worked-arrow\\">\\r\\n        <svg width=\\"15\\" height=\\"15\\" viewBox=\\"0 0 15 15\\" fill=\\"none\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\" style=\\"flex:none;\\"><path d=\\"M7.5 1.5v12M3 9l4.5 4.5L12 9\\" stroke=\\"var(--classroom-link)\\" stroke-width=\\"1.5\\" stroke-linecap=\\"round\\" stroke-linejoin=\\"round\\"/></svg>\\r\\n        <span>enter &rarr; pass</span>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n    <details>\\r\\n      <summary><span class=\\"cs-disc\\"><svg class=\\"cs-chev\\" width=\\"9\\" height=\\"11\\" viewBox=\\"0 0 9 11\\" fill=\\"none\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\"><path d=\\"M1.5 1L7 5.5 1.5 10\\" stroke=\\"var(--classroom-link-text)\\" stroke-width=\\"1.6\\" stroke-linecap=\\"round\\" stroke-linejoin=\\"round\\"/></svg><span>All four, each in a sentence</span></span></summary>\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-panel\\">\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-word\\">unless</div><div class=\\"cs-example\\"><div class=\\"cs-example-sentence\\">You cannot enter unless you have a pass.</div><div class=\\"cs-example-arrow\\">enter &rarr; pass</div></div></div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-word\\">until</div><div class=\\"cs-example\\"><div class=\\"cs-example-sentence\\">You cannot leave until the bell rings.</div><div class=\\"cs-example-arrow\\">leave &rarr; bell rang</div></div></div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-word\\">without</div><div class=\\"cs-example\\"><div class=\\"cs-example-sentence\\">No one enters without a badge.</div><div class=\\"cs-example-arrow\\">enter &rarr; badge</div></div></div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-word\\">except</div><div class=\\"cs-example\\"><div class=\\"cs-example-sentence\\">Everyone may enter except minors.</div><div class=\\"cs-example-arrow\\">enter &rarr; NOT a minor</div></div></div>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n    </details>\\r\\n  </div>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <!-- NEGATE RIGHT -->\\r\\n  <div class=\\"cs-family\\" id=\\"cs-negright\\">\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-tagline\\"><span class=\\"cs-tag\\">NEGATE RIGHT</span><span class=\\"cs-count\\">5 words</span></div>\\r\\n    <h2 class=\\"cs-h2\\">Words that negate the right</h2>\\r\\n    <p class=\\"cs-why\\">The attached idea is the trigger and goes left, but the negative belongs to the second half of the sentence.</p>\\r\\n    <p class=\\"cs-ask\\">Rewrite it as a positive before you draw and it stops being confusing: \\"no A are B\\" is \\"all A are not B.\\"</p>\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-worked\\">\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-worked-sentence\\">No freshman may apply.</div>\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-worked-arrow\\">\\r\\n        <svg width=\\"15\\" height=\\"15\\" viewBox=\\"0 0 15 15\\" fill=\\"none\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\" style=\\"flex:none;\\"><path d=\\"M7.5 1.5v12M3 9l4.5 4.5L12 9\\" stroke=\\"var(--classroom-link)\\" stroke-width=\\"1.5\\" stroke-linecap=\\"round\\" stroke-linejoin=\\"round\\"/></svg>\\r\\n        <span>freshman &rarr; NOT apply</span>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n    <details>\\r\\n      <summary><span class=\\"cs-disc\\"><svg class=\\"cs-chev\\" width=\\"9\\" height=\\"11\\" viewBox=\\"0 0 9 11\\" fill=\\"none\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\"><path d=\\"M1.5 1L7 5.5 1.5 10\\" stroke=\\"var(--classroom-link-text)\\" stroke-width=\\"1.6\\" stroke-linecap=\\"round\\" stroke-linejoin=\\"round\\"/></svg><span>All five, each in a sentence</span></span></summary>\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-panel\\">\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-word\\">no</div><div class=\\"cs-example\\"><div class=\\"cs-example-sentence\\">No freshman may apply.</div><div class=\\"cs-example-arrow\\">freshman &rarr; NOT apply</div></div></div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-word\\">none</div><div class=\\"cs-example\\"><div class=\\"cs-example-sentence\\">None of the drafts were saved.</div><div class=\\"cs-example-arrow\\">draft &rarr; NOT saved</div></div></div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-word\\">never</div><div class=\\"cs-example\\"><div class=\\"cs-example-sentence\\">A draft is never final.</div><div class=\\"cs-example-arrow\\">draft &rarr; NOT final</div></div></div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-word\\">cannot</div><div class=\\"cs-example\\"><div class=\\"cs-example-sentence\\">A member cannot vote twice.</div><div class=\\"cs-example-arrow\\">member &rarr; NOT vote twice</div></div></div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-word\\">not</div><div class=\\"cs-example\\"><div class=\\"cs-example-sentence\\">Paper applications are not accepted.</div><div class=\\"cs-example-arrow\\">paper application &rarr; NOT accepted</div></div></div>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n    </details>\\r\\n  </div>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <!-- NO LIST -->\\r\\n  <div class=\\"cs-family\\" id=\\"cs-nolist\\">\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-tagline\\"><span class=\\"cs-tag\\">NO LIST</span><span class=\\"cs-count\\">the ones you meet cold</span></div>\\r\\n    <h2 class=\\"cs-h2\\">Words that are on no list</h2>\\r\\n    <p class=\\"cs-why\\">Some words have a home in the lists above. Others you will meet cold. Either way the same question settles it: what is guaranteed, and what is required.</p>\\r\\n    <p class=\\"cs-ask\\">Whatever sets the rule off goes left. Whatever has to be there goes right. That works whether the word made a list or not.</p>\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-worked\\">\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-worked-sentence\\">Spotting the gap is a prerequisite to picking the assumption.</div>\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-worked-arrow\\">\\r\\n        <svg width=\\"15\\" height=\\"15\\" viewBox=\\"0 0 15 15\\" fill=\\"none\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\" style=\\"flex:none;\\"><path d=\\"M7.5 1.5v12M3 9l4.5 4.5L12 9\\" stroke=\\"var(--classroom-link)\\" stroke-width=\\"1.5\\" stroke-linecap=\\"round\\" stroke-linejoin=\\"round\\"/></svg>\\r\\n        <span>pick assumption &rarr; spot gap</span>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n    <details>\\r\\n      <summary><span class=\\"cs-disc\\"><svg class=\\"cs-chev\\" width=\\"9\\" height=\\"11\\" viewBox=\\"0 0 9 11\\" fill=\\"none\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\"><path d=\\"M1.5 1L7 5.5 1.5 10\\" stroke=\\"var(--classroom-link-text)\\" stroke-width=\\"1.6\\" stroke-linecap=\\"round\\" stroke-linejoin=\\"round\\"/></svg><span>Four that come up</span></span></summary>\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-panel\\">\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-word\\">prerequisite</div><div class=\\"cs-example\\"><div class=\\"cs-example-sentence\\">A requirement, so it goes right.</div><div class=\\"cs-example-arrow\\">physics &rarr; calculus</div></div></div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-word\\">depends on</div><div class=\\"cs-example\\"><div class=\\"cs-example-sentence\\">A requirement, so it goes right.</div><div class=\\"cs-example-arrow\\">picnic &rarr; good weather</div></div></div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-word\\">will</div><div class=\\"cs-example\\"><div class=\\"cs-example-sentence\\">An outcome, so it goes right too.</div><div class=\\"cs-example-arrow\\">deadline passes &rarr; form locks</div></div></div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-word\\">the moment that</div><div class=\\"cs-example\\"><div class=\\"cs-example-sentence\\">A trigger, so it goes left.</div><div class=\\"cs-example-arrow\\">the moment it ships &rarr; billing starts</div></div></div>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n    </details>\\r\\n  </div>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <!-- FLIP -->\\r\\n  <div class=\\"cs-family\\" id=\\"cs-flip\\">\\r\\n    <h2 class=\\"cs-h2\\">Flipping a rule</h2>\\r\\n    <p class=\\"cs-why\\" style=\\"margin-bottom:24px;\\">A rule and its flip are two sides of the same coin. Flipping is what the test is really checking, and the third step is where the points are.</p>\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-figure\\">\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-flip-row\\">\\r\\n        <span class=\\"cs-flip-term\\">A and B &rarr; C</span>\\r\\n        <svg width=\\"52\\" height=\\"20\\" viewBox=\\"0 0 52 20\\" fill=\\"none\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\" style=\\"flex:none;\\"><path d=\\"M2 10h42M38 4l7 6-7 6\\" stroke=\\"var(--classroom-link)\\" stroke-width=\\"2\\" stroke-linecap=\\"round\\" stroke-linejoin=\\"round\\"/></svg>\\r\\n        <span class=\\"cs-flip-term\\">NOT C &rarr; NOT A or NOT B</span>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-grid-3\\">\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-step\\"><div class=\\"cs-step-title\\">Swap</div><div class=\\"cs-step-body\\">What was on the right goes on the left.</div></div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-step\\"><div class=\\"cs-step-title\\">Negate</div><div class=\\"cs-step-body\\">Positive becomes negative, negative becomes positive.</div></div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-step\\"><div class=\\"cs-step-title\\">Switch the connector</div><div class=\\"cs-step-body\\">Every and becomes an or, every or becomes an and.</div></div>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n      <p class=\\"cs-caption\\">If C did not happen, at least one of A or B is missing, and you cannot say which. Writing <span class=\\"cs-mono\\">and</span> there claims both are missing, which is more than the rule ever gave you.</p>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n  </div>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <!-- USE -->\\r\\n  <div class=\\"cs-family\\" id=\\"cs-use\\">\\r\\n    <h2 class=\\"cs-h2\\">The two things you do with a rule</h2>\\r\\n    <p class=\\"cs-why\\" style=\\"margin-bottom:24px;\\">Everything above gets the arrow onto the page. From there the test only ever asks for two things.</p>\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-grid-2\\">\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-card\\">\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-card-title\\" style=\\"font-size:18px;\\">Chain it</div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-notation\\">rule + rule = a new rule</div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-card-gloss\\">If the right side of one rule is the left side of another, they link, and the link is the inference.</div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-mono\\" style=\\"font-size:14.5px; color:var(--classroom-link-text); text-align:center;\\">A &rarr; B &rarr; C &rarr; D</div>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-card\\">\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-card-title\\" style=\\"font-size:18px;\\">Trigger it</div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-notation\\">rule + fact = a new fact</div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-card-gloss\\">A rule sits there doing nothing until a fact sets it off. Hand it the left side and the right side has to follow.</div>\\r\\n        <div class=\\"cs-mono\\" style=\\"font-size:14.5px; color:var(--classroom-link-text); text-align:center;\\">A &rarr; B or C, plus A, plus NOT C</div>\\r\\n      </div>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n  </div>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <h2 class=\\"cs-eyebrowless\\" style=\\"margin-bottom:18px;\\">The whole thing, in five steps</h2>\\r\\n  <div>\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-step-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-num\\">01</div><div class=\\"cs-step-text\\">Find the word. One word tells you which side is the trigger and which side is required.</div></div>\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-step-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-num\\">02</div><div class=\\"cs-step-text\\">Do what it says. Left, right, negate left, or negate right.</div></div>\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-step-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-num\\">03</div><div class=\\"cs-step-text\\">Flip it when the question wants the other direction. Swap, negate, and switch every and to an or.</div></div>\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-step-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-num\\">04</div><div class=\\"cs-step-text\\">Chain it or trigger it. Rule plus rule gives a new rule. Rule plus fact gives a new fact.</div></div>\\r\\n    <div class=\\"cs-step-row\\"><div class=\\"cs-num\\">05</div><div class=\\"cs-step-text\\">Only draw when the rules could link. A single conditional inside an ordinary argument is something you hear, not something you write down.</div></div>\\r\\n  </div>\\r\\n\\r\\n  <div class=\\"cs-next\\">\\r\\n    <div style=\\"flex:1;\\">\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-next-title\\">The Breakdown</div>\\r\\n      <div class=\\"cs-next-sub\\">Where each of these gets taught properly, one part at a time.</div>\\r\\n    </div>\\r\\n    <svg width=\\"20\\" height=\\"14\\" viewBox=\\"0 0 20 14\\" fill=\\"none\\" aria-hidden=\\"true\\" style=\\"flex:none;\\"><path d=\\"M1 7h16M13 2l5 5-5 5\\" stroke=\\"var(--classroom-link-text)\\" stroke-width=\\"1.7\\" stroke-linecap=\\"round\\" stroke-linejoin=\\"round\\"/></svg>\\r\\n  </div>\\r\\n\\r\\n</div>\\r\\n\\r\\n"}'}]

The cheat sheet tells you what to write. This tells you why it is right. The arrow is certainty. If the left side is true, the right side has to be true. Everything below is a consequence of that one sentence. 01 Words that put it on the left 19 words 02 Words that put it on the right 16 words 03 Words that negate the left 4 words 04 Words that negate the right 5 words 05 Words that are on no list the cold ones 06 And, or, and the flip the three steps 07 Both directions at once biconditionals 08 Not conditionals at all few, not all 09 The two moves that are never allowed the illegal ones 10 The two things you do with a rule chain, trigger 11 When to draw at all setup, not answer 01 Words that put it on the left These are rules, universals and guarantees. All three say the same thing: when this, then that. Whatever the word attaches to is the trigger. It goes on the left, exactly as written, and nothing gets negated. It shows up like this If A, then B. B whenever A. Every A is B. All A are B. Ask yourself \\"If what?\\" or \\"every who or what?\\" Whatever answers it goes on the left. Which gives you If left, right. right if left. If it rains, practice is cancelled. rain → practice cancelled All 19 words, each in a sentence if If it rains, practice is cancelled. rain → practice cancelled when When the alarm sounds, the doors lock. alarm sounds → doors lock whenever Whenever she drills, she reviews it after. drills → reviews after wherever Wherever the trail floods, it closes. trail floods → trail closes anytime Anytime the server restarts, you sign in again. server restarts → sign in again provided that You may retake it provided that you wait 30 days. waited 30 days → may retake in the event that In the event that the flight is cancelled, you get a refund. flight cancelled → refund all All members pay dues. member → pays dues any Any late paper loses points. late paper → loses points every Every witness was interviewed. witness → interviewed each Each application needs a signature. application → needs signature everyone Everyone who enters signs in. enters → signs in everything Everything on the shelf is discounted. on the shelf → discounted leads to A tie in the vote leads to a runoff. tie → runoff produces Heating the rail produces expansion. heat the rail → expansion results in Missing the deadline results in disqualification. miss deadline → disqualified guarantees A winning ticket guarantees the prize. winning ticket → prize ensures A signed receipt ensures the delivery was logged. signed receipt → delivery logged always A missed payment always adds a late fee. missed payment → late fee Diagram this. If you score a 160, you get the scholarship. Check it 160 → scholarship The idea the word attaches to goes left, word for word. No rearranging, no negating. 02 Words that put it on the right These are requirements and implications. The word names what has to be there, so that idea goes on the right, exactly as written. Same sentence as the one above, one word different, and the arrow points the other way. It shows up like this A only if B. Only A are B. A requires B. B is necessary for A. Ask yourself \\"What is this word naming as required?\\" Whatever answers it goes on the right. Which gives you left only if right. right is required for left. You pass only if you attend. pass → attend All 16 words, each in a sentence only Only members may vote. vote → member only if You pass only if you attend. pass → attend only when The gate opens only when the light is green. gate opens → light green requires Admission requires a transcript. admission → transcript required A permit is required to build. build → permit prerequisite Calculus is a prerequisite for physics. physics → calculus must Applicants must submit two essays. applicant → two essays needs The plant needs light to bloom. blooms → light essential Oxygen is essential to combustion. combustion → oxygen necessary A quorum is necessary for a vote. vote → quorum depends on The picnic depends on good weather. picnic → good weather then If the bill passes, then it becomes law. bill passes → becomes law implies An even number implies a factor of 2. even → factor of 2 signals A siren signals an emergency. siren → emergency means A red tag means it is sold. red tag → sold will If the deadline passes, the form will lock. deadline passes → form locks Diagram this. You get the scholarship only if you score a 160. Check it scholarship → 160 One word separates this from the sentence above, and the arrow reverses. Slow down every time you see only and ask what it is marking as required. 03 Words that negate the left All four are \\"if not\\" in disguise. The attached idea is what is required, so it goes right, and the other side picks up a NOT. If you would rather not negate anything, swap the word for \\"if not\\" and put that on the left instead. Both routes give the same rule, one as the flip of the other. It shows up like this A unless B. No A without B. Not A until B. Ask yourself Read the word as \\"if not\\", then put that on the left and negate it. Which gives you left unless right. Or: if not right, then not left. You cannot enter unless you have a pass. enter → pass All four, each in a sentence unless You cannot enter unless you have a pass. enter → pass until You cannot leave until the bell rings. leave → bell rang without No one enters without a badge. enter → badge except Everyone may enter except minors. enter → NOT a minor Diagram this. You cannot submit the form without a signature. Check it submit → signature Which is the same rule as NOT signature → NOT submit. The two routes always give you a rule and its flip, so if yours came out as the other one, you are still right. 04 Words that negate the right The attached idea is the trigger and goes left, but the negative belongs to the second half of the sentence. Rewrite it as a positive before you draw and it stops being confusing: \\"no A are B\\" is \\"all A are not B.\\" It shows up like this No A is B. None of the A are B. A is never B. A cannot be B. Ask yourself Rewrite it as a positive first, then draw what you rewrote. Which gives you No left is right. Which is left → NOT right. No freshman may apply. freshman → NOT apply All five, each in a sentence no No freshman may apply. freshman → NOT apply none None of the drafts were saved. draft → NOT saved never A draft is never final. draft → NOT final cannot A member cannot vote twice. member → NOT vote twice not Paper applications are not accepted. paper application → NOT accepted Diagram this. No expired badge opens the door. Check it expired badge → NOT opens door The NOT sits on the right. Putting it on the left is the most common way this group goes wrong. 05 Words that are on no list Some words have a home in the lists above. Others you will meet cold. Either way the same question settles it: what is guaranteed, and what is required. Whatever sets the rule off goes left. Whatever has to be there goes right. That works whether the word made a list or not. Learning to walk is a prerequisite to running. Check it running → learned to walk Learning to walk is the requirement, so it goes on the right, even though it happens first. 06 And, or, and the flip A rule and its flip are two sides of the same coin. The flip says: when the right side is not true, the left side cannot be true either. Flipping a rule is what the test is really checking. Three steps, and the third is where the points are. A and B → C NOT C → NOT A or NOT B 01 Swap What was on the right goes on the left. 02 Negate Positive becomes negative, negative becomes positive. 03 Switch the connector Every and becomes an or, every or becomes an and. If C did not happen, at least one of A or B is missing, and you cannot say which. Writing and there claims both are missing, which is more than the rule ever gave you. Going the other way, A → B or C becomes NOT B and NOT C → NOT A . 07 Both directions at once Some sentences hand you the rule going both ways. Two words do it, and they do not work the same way. if and only if The if puts it left and the only if puts it right, so it is telling you to do both. Also then and only then, when and only when. otherwise It hands you a second rule covering the other case. Write both down, flip both, and the arrow runs both ways. Either both happen or neither does. Why otherwise runs both ways Jeff takes a vacation if he wins the lottery. Otherwise, he does not. That is two rules, not one. lottery → vacation NOT lottery → NOT vacation Flip both and they collapse into a double arrow. The trap: one rule plus its own flip is not this. The deposit clears, otherwise the seat is released gives you only NOT deposit → released . Flipping that is the same rule said backwards, not a second direction. The sentence has to state both cases. 08 Not conditionals at all These sit next to no and behave nothing like it. They count. They do not guarantee, so do not draw an arrow. Few A are B is most A are not B Not all A are B is some A are not B Not all the essays were graded tells you at least one was not graded. An arrow would say every essay behaves the same way, which is more than the sentence gave you. Flip these to the positive and leave them as statements. 09 The two moves that are never allowed Given A → B , four things can happen and only two of them prove anything. When the trigger is missing the rule simply disappears. It has no power over you, so it tells you nothing. A is true B has to be true. B is false A cannot be true. A is false Nothing follows. Saying B is false is the illegal move. B is true Nothing follows. Saying A is true is reading the arrow backwards. Try it. The rule is if the alarm sounds, the doors lock. You are told the doors are locked. What follows? Check it Nothing follows. The doors could be locked for any number of reasons. You were handed the right side and asked to claim the left. When a flaw answer says the author confuses a necessary condition with a sufficient one, this is what it is describing. 10 The two things you do with a rule Everything above gets the arrow onto the page. From there the test only ever asks for two things. Chain it to another rule and you get a new rule. Trigger it with a fact and you get a new fact. Chaining Whenever you have more than one conditional, ask one question: do these connect? If the right side of one rule is the left side of another, they link, and the link is the inference. A → B → C → D flips whole NOT D → NOT C → NOT B → NOT A Start at the far end and walk it backwards. The test usually wants the front joined to the back, so A → D is the headline answer. But every shorter run is an inference too: A → C , B → D , NOT C → NOT A . Triggering A rule sits there doing nothing until a fact sets it off. Hand it the left side and the right side has to follow. Hand it a missing piece of the right side and the rest has to carry the load. rule A → B or C fact A fact NOT C so B Which one is being asked for? Look at the shape of the answers. If they read if this, then that , you are chaining and the answer is a rule. If they read as a flat statement, you are triggering and the answer is a fact. 11 When to draw at all Diagramming is setup, not the answer. Draw only when there is a reason to. DRAW Two or more conditionals that could link. Chain them and look for what connects the two ends. DRAW The conclusion is itself a conditional. The author is claiming one thing guarantees another. DO NOT One conditional in an ordinary argument. Hear it as a requirement and keep reading. The missing link: an argument gives you A → B and concludes A → C . There is a hole in the chain, and the piece that fills it is B → C , or its flip, and that piece is the answer. Test yourself Say the answer out loud before you open each one, and you will know which part still needs work. unless Negate left. The attached idea goes right, the other side takes a NOT. Or read it as if not and put that on the left. only if Right. Whatever follows it goes on the right, exactly as written. The three steps of the flip Swap the sides, negate both, and switch every and to an or. You are given the right side of a rule. What follows? Nothing. Claiming the left from the right is reading the arrow backwards, and it is the most commonly tested flaw on the test. few A are B Not a conditional. It means most A are not B. It counts, it does not guarantee, so no arrow. A → B, B → C, C → D. What is the flip of the whole chain? NOT D → NOT C → NOT B → NOT A. Start at the far end and walk it backwards. The headline inference is A → D, but every shorter run counts too. Rule plus rule, and rule plus fact. What does each give you? Rule plus rule chains into a new rule. Rule plus fact triggers a new fact. The shape of the answer choices tells you which one is being asked for. When is it worth drawing? When two or more rules could link, or when the conclusion is itself a conditional. A single conditional in an ordinary argument is something you hear, not something you write down. \\r\\n"}'}]
